People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hello, Doctor. Well, I suppose I came in because... I just can’t shake this feeling at work. I teach secondary school, and lately, certain staff emails and the morning announcements... they really feel like they're coded messages about me. 

The announcements are over the PA system, which honestly has been acting up since last spring. I keep telling the principal we need a new intercom, but the school budget is always so tight. Just last week, I had to buy my own dry-erase markers again because the supply closet was completely empty. It’s really ridiculous how much out-of-pocket spending we teachers have to do these days. I mean, when I was getting my master's and first started teaching, the PTA would at least fund the basic classroom supplies. Now you're lucky if you get a box of tissues. 

But anyway... where was I? Oh, right, the messages. Yes, it's just this overwhelming sense I get. Even when my colleagues gently show me that the emails were just standard memos sent to everyone on the staff list, I still feel like there's a hidden meaning in them meant specifically for me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose I have. Sometimes when it's really quiet, like after the classroom finally empties out for the day... which, honestly, takes forever because the kids just linger in the hallways now. They don't go straight to their lockers or the buses like they used to. They just stand around staring at their phones. I had to confiscate three phones just during my second-period class yesterday. It's school policy, but the parents get so upset with us when we take them away, as if we're the ones breaking the rules! I had a mother email me last month absolutely furious because her daughter couldn't text her during a quiz. 

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, hearing things. Yes, when the room is finally empty, or when I'm just in the quiet of my own house, I do hear a woman's voice. She'll just say my name, or make a short comment about what I'm doing at that moment. It sounds so completely clear that I actually answer her out loud before I realize no one is there. It happens when I'm grading, or cooking... I make a really wonderful pot roast, by the way. It's my grandmother's recipe. She used to cook it in this heavy cast-iron Dutch oven that I inherited from her. Cast iron is so heavy and hard to clean, though, you can't use regular dish soap on it or it completely ruins the seasoning...

I think the worries about the coded messages and hearing that woman's voice... it's been going on for about four months now. It definitely didn't happen all at once. It was more of a gradual thing, building up over the semester. \n\nWhich, honestly, has been the longest semester of my life. The administration decided to change our entire grading software right in the middle of October. Can you imagine? Just as midterms were approaching! We all had to sit through this dreadful four-hour professional development seminar on a Friday afternoon to learn how to use it. The presenter just read off his slides the entire time in this monotone voice. I had to drink three cups of that terrible teachers' lounge coffee just to stay awake. You know the kind—it tastes like it's been sitting on the burner since 1998 and it's practically sludge at the bottom of the pot. I usually bring my own French press, but I had forgotten it that morning because I was rushing to get out the door. The traffic on I-95 has been an absolute nightmare lately with all the construction... \n\nBut yes, about four months. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn't say I've felt unusually energetic or anything like that. I mean, who has the energy for new projects these days anyway? I'm exhausted by the time I get home. Between the grading and the lesson planning, there's just no time. 

Speaking of projects, though, the school decided to completely overhaul the science fair this year. They want us to integrate it with the arts department—some sort of "STEAM" initiative instead of just STEM. It's a nice idea in theory, I suppose, but the execution has been a complete disaster. The art teacher, Brenda, she's lovely, really, but she is so disorganized. We were supposed to have a joint planning meeting on Tuesday, and she completely forgot and went to get her car's oil changed instead. Which I understand, car maintenance is important. My old Subaru has been making this rattling noise every time I go over 40 miles an hour. I took it to the mechanic on Elm Street—the one with the big neon wrench sign—and he said it was the catalytic converter. Do you have any idea how much it costs to replace one of those? It's astronomical. And with the price of groceries right now, I just don't know how they expect people to manage. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's like they're made of gold. I used to bake all the time, but now I have to think twice before making a simple pound cake...

But no, I haven't been feeling energetic or taking on new things. Just trying to keep up with the usual workload.

Sad or empty? No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not crying all the time or anything like that. And I still enjoy my usual things. I still go to my book club on Thursday evenings. We're reading this historical fiction novel set in 1920s London right now. Though, honestly, Mary—she's the one who usually hosts—she always chooses these incredibly long, dense books. Last month it was an 800-page biography of some obscure British politician. Who has the time to read 800 pages? 

Especially with the state of the school library right now. They decided to pull up all the old carpeting, and the dust is just everywhere in the building. My allergies have been acting up terribly because of it. I've been buying those expensive air filters for my classroom, the HEPA ones, but they only do so much when the school's HVAC system is practically ancient. The maintenance guy, Frank, told me the ducts haven't been properly cleaned since the late eighties. Can you imagine what we're breathing in every day? It's a wonder half the staff doesn't have chronic bronchitis. Frank says he keeps putting in work orders to get professionals out here to clean them, but the district keeps denying the budget request. They always say there's no money, but then they just bought brand new uniforms for the football team... 

But anyway, what was I saying? No, my mood has been fine. Just the normal amount of stress, really.

Well, sleep... I suppose I get about six or seven hours a night, usually. I'm not really sure if I feel rested, to be honest. It's hard to tell when my alarm goes off at 5:30 every morning. 

Which is just an ungodly hour to be awake, really. But I have to get up that early because the school decided to change the bell schedule this year. We start first period at 7:15 now. 7:15! Teenagers shouldn't even be conscious at that hour, let alone trying to learn algebra. I read an article in the Times last week—or maybe it was the Post, I can't remember—anyway, it was about circadian rhythms in adolescents. It said their brains are literally wired to stay up late and sleep in. So we're fighting biology every single morning. And you can see it in their faces. Half of them are practically comatose until third period. 

I try to keep the lights a bit dim in my homeroom just to ease them into the day, but then the principal, Mr. Henderson, did a walkthrough last week and told me it was "too gloomy." He's always on about creating a "vibrant learning environment." Easy for him to say from his office with the big windows and the fancy ergonomic chair. He just ordered himself one of those standing desks, too. The ones that go up and down with a little motor. Meanwhile, my desk chair has a wobbly wheel that squeaks every time I lean to the left. I've put in three maintenance requests for it since September, but Frank—the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—he says they're backordered. Everything is backordered these days, isn't it? I tried to order some new garden shears from that catalog that comes in the mail, the one with the nice glossy pages, and they told me it would be six to eight weeks...

But sleep, yes. I get about six or seven hours. I don't know if I feel rested, I just get up and get going.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, nobody has really said I'm withdrawn, exactly, but... I don't know, it's like I still care about my students and my friends, obviously. But for months now, it just feels like the warmth and the drive have completely drained out of me. I just let things slide unless someone really pushes me. I haven't been going to book club, and I stepped back from the theater planning committee. Even making a simple phone call feels like climbing a mountain. 

Speaking of mountains, my sister went to Colorado last month. She sent me all these pictures of the Rockies. They looked beautiful, all covered in snow. She stayed in this little cabin that had a wood-burning stove, which sounds so cozy, doesn't it? I haven't had a real fire since I lived in that apartment on 4th Street back in the nineties. It had this lovely exposed brick wall and a real fireplace, but the draft in the winter was just terrible. You had to put plastic wrap over the windows just to keep the heat in. My landlord back then, Mr. Peterson, he was entirely useless. He'd just tell you to put on a sweater. I remember I bought this thick, ugly wool sweater from a thrift shop just to survive February... 

But yes, I suppose I haven't been doing as much lately. The energy just isn't there for it.

graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Over these past four months while you've been hearing the voice and feeling like the messages are about you, have you noticed a significant change in your mood—such as feeling persistently sad, empty, or hopeless? Or have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not crying all the time or anything like that. And I still enjoy my usual things. I still go to my book club on Thursday evenings. We're reading this historical fiction novel set in 1920s London right now. Though, honestly, Mary—she's the one who usually hosts—she always chooses these incredibly long, dense books. Last month it was an 800-page biography of some obscure British politician. Who has the time to read 800 pages? \n\nEspecially with the state of the school library right now. They decided to pull up all the old carpeting, and the dust is just everywhere in the building. My allergies have been acting up terribly because of it. I've been buying those expensive air filters for my classroom, the HEPA ones, but they only do so much when the school's HVAC system is practically ancient. The maintenance guy, Frank, told me the ducts haven't been properly cleaned since the late eighties. Can you imagine what we're breathing in every day? It's a wonder half the staff doesn't have chronic bronchitis. Frank says he keeps putting in work orders to get professionals out here to clean them, but the district keeps denying the budget request. They always say there's no money, but then they just bought brand new uniforms for the football team... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? No, my mood has been fine. Just the normal amount of stress, really.\n\nDoctor: When you do sleep at night, how many hours would you say you're typically sleeping? And do you find that you feel rested after that amount of sleep, or do you wake up feeling like you need more?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... I suppose I get about six or seven hours a night, usually. I'm not really sure if I feel rested, to be honest. It's hard to tell when my alarm goes off at 5:30 every morning. \n\nWhich is just an ungodly hour to be awake, really. But I have to get up that early because the school decided to change the bell schedule this year. We start first period at 7:15 now. 7:15! Teenagers shouldn't even be conscious at that hour, let alone trying to learn algebra. I read an article in the Times last week—or maybe it was the Post, I can't remember—anyway, it was about circadian rhythms in adolescents. It said their brains are literally wired to stay up late and sleep in. So we're fighting biology every single morning. And you can see it in their faces. Half of them are practically comatose until third period. \n\nI try to keep the lights a bit dim in my homeroom just to ease them into the day, but then the principal, Mr. Henderson, did a walkthrough last week and told me it was \"too gloomy.\" He's always on about creating a \"vibrant learning environment.\" Easy for him to say from his office with the big windows and the fancy ergonomic chair. He just ordered himself one of those standing desks, too. The ones that go up and down with a little motor. Meanwhile, my desk chair has a wobbly wheel that squeaks every time I lean to the left. I've put in three maintenance requests for it since September, but Frank—the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—he says they're backordered. Everything is backordered these days, isn't it? I tried to order some new garden shears from that catalog that comes in the mail, the one with the nice glossy pages, and they told me it would be six to eight weeks...\n\nBut sleep, yes. I get about six or seven hours. I don't know if I feel rested, I just get up and get going.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking with colleagues or family members, do people ever comment that you seem withdrawn, or that you're not expressing emotions as much as you normally would? Or have you noticed that you're having trouble organizing your thoughts or finding the right words to say?\n\nPatient: Well, nobody has really said I'm withdrawn, exactly, but... I don't know, it's like I still care about my students and my friends, obviously. But for months now, it just feels like the warmth and the drive have completely drained out of me. I just let things slide unless someone really pushes me. I haven't been going to book club, and I stepped back from the theater planning committee. Even making a simple phone call feels like climbing a mountain. \n\nSpeaking of mountains, my sister went to Colorado last month. She sent me all these pictures of the Rockies. They looked beautiful, all covered in snow. She stayed in this little cabin that had a wood-burning stove, which sounds so cozy, doesn't it? I haven't had a real fire since I lived in that apartment on 4th Street back in the nineties. It had this lovely exposed brick wall and a real fireplace, but the draft in the winter was just terrible. You had to put plastic wrap over the windows just to keep the heat in. My landlord back then, Mr. Peterson, he was entirely useless. He'd just tell you to put on a sweater. I remember I bought this thick, ugly wool sweater from a thrift shop just to survive February... \n\nBut yes, I suppose I haven't been doing as much lately. The energy just isn't there for it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these four months, or even before that, have you ever had a period—maybe lasting a week or two or longer—where you needed significantly less sleep than usual but still felt energetic and rested, and where you were accomplishing a lot of things or felt like you could do much more than you normally can?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't really had anything like that. I don't think I've ever felt like I didn't need sleep, or had some sudden burst of energy to do a million things. 

Actually, energy is something I've been thinking about a lot lately, especially with the electric bill going up again. They sent a notice last week saying the rates are increasing by eight percent starting in January. Eight percent! And they blame it on infrastructure upgrades, but you look around and the power lines in my neighborhood are still sagging into the oak trees. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's been calling the city for three years to come trim the branches away from the wires near her driveway. She's terrified that in the next big storm, a branch is going to come down and start a fire. She even bought one of those heavy-duty fire extinguishers for her garage, the kind they use in commercial kitchens. It's bright yellow, not red, which I thought was interesting. I always thought they had to be red by law, but apparently not. I saw one at the hardware store when I went to buy some weather stripping for my back door. The draft in my kitchen is just awful this time of year. You stand by the sink to wash dishes and your ankles absolutely freeze...

But no, to answer your question, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need to sleep. Just the opposite lately, really.

I teach secondary school, and lately, certain staff emails and the morning announcements... they really feel like they're coded messages about me. \n\nThe announcements are over the PA system, which honestly has been acting up since last spring. I keep telling the principal we need a new intercom, but the school budget is always so tight. Just last week, I had to buy my own dry-erase markers again because the supply closet was completely empty. It’s really ridiculous how much out-of-pocket spending we teachers have to do these days. I mean, when I was getting my master's and first started teaching, the PTA would at least fund the basic classroom supplies. Now you're lucky if you get a box of tissues. \n\nBut anyway... where was I? Oh, right, the messages. Yes, it's just this overwhelming sense I get. Even when my colleagues gently show me that the emails were just standard memos sent to everyone on the staff list, I still feel like there's a hidden meaning in them meant specifically for me.\n\nDoctor: Besides these feelings about the emails and announcements being about you, have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people around you couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Sometimes when it's really quiet, like after the classroom finally empties out for the day... which, honestly, takes forever because the kids just linger in the hallways now. They don't go straight to their lockers or the buses like they used to. They just stand around staring at their phones. I had to confiscate three phones just during my second-period class yesterday. It's school policy, but the parents get so upset with us when we take them away, as if we're the ones breaking the rules! I had a mother email me last month absolutely furious because her daughter couldn't text her during a quiz. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, hearing things. Yes, when the room is finally empty, or when I'm just in the quiet of my own house, I do hear a woman's voice. She'll just say my name, or make a short comment about what I'm doing at that moment. It sounds so completely clear that I actually answer her out loud before I realize no one is there. It happens when I'm grading, or cooking... I make a really wonderful pot roast, by the way. It's my grandmother's recipe. She used to cook it in this heavy cast-iron Dutch oven that I inherited from her. Cast iron is so heavy and hard to clean, though, you can't use regular dish soap on it or it completely ruins the seasoning...\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings that the emails and announcements were about you, and when did you first hear the woman's voice? Was this something that came on suddenly over a few days, or has it been building up gradually over weeks or months?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... I think the worries about the coded messages and hearing that woman's voice... it's been going on for about four months now. It definitely didn't happen all at once. It was more of a gradual thing, building up over the semester. \n\nWhich, honestly, has been the longest semester of my life. The administration decided to change our entire grading software right in the middle of October. Can you imagine? Just as midterms were approaching! We all had to sit through this dreadful four-hour professional development seminar on a Friday afternoon to learn how to use it. The presenter just read off his slides the entire time in this monotone voice. I had to drink three cups of that terrible teachers' lounge coffee just to stay awake. You know the kind—it tastes like it's been sitting on the burner since 1998 and it's practically sludge at the bottom of the pot. I usually bring my own French press, but I had forgotten it that morning because I was rushing to get out the door. The traffic on I-95 has been an absolute nightmare lately with all the construction... \n\nBut yes, about four months. It just sort of slowly crept up on me.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you noticed any periods where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than normal, or found yourself taking on many new projects or activities with a lot of enthusiasm?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've felt unusually energetic or anything like that. I mean, who has the energy for new projects these days anyway? I'm exhausted by the time I get home. Between the grading and the lesson planning, there's just no time. \n\nSpeaking of projects, though, the school decided to completely overhaul the science fair this year. They want us to integrate it with the arts department—some sort of \"STEAM\" initiative instead of just STEM. It's a nice idea in theory, I suppose, but the execution has been a complete disaster. The art teacher, Brenda, she's lovely, really, but she is so disorganized. We were supposed to have a joint planning meeting on Tuesday, and she completely forgot and went to get her car's oil changed instead. Which I understand, car maintenance is important. My old Subaru has been making this rattling noise every time I go over 40 miles an hour. I took it to the mechanic on Elm Street—the one with the big neon wrench sign—and he said it was the catalytic converter. Do you have any idea how much it costs to replace one of those? It's astronomical. And with the price of groceries right now, I just don't know how they expect people to manage. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's like they're made of gold. I used to bake all the time, but now I have to think twice before making a simple pound cake...\n\nBut no, I haven't been feeling energetic or taking on new things. Just trying to keep up with the usual workload.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months while you've been hearing the voice and feeling like the messages are about you, have you noticed a significant change in your mood—such as feeling persistently sad, empty, or hopeless? Or have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not crying all the time or anything like that. And I still enjoy my usual things. I still go to my book club on Thursday evenings. We're reading this historical fiction novel set in 1920s London right now. Though, honestly, Mary—she's the one who usually hosts—she always chooses these incredibly long, dense books. Last month it was an 800-page biography of some obscure British politician. Who has the time to read 800 pages? \n\nEspecially with the state of the school library right now. They decided to pull up all the old carpeting, and the dust is just everywhere in the building. My allergies have been acting up terribly because of it. I've been buying those expensive air filters for my classroom, the HEPA ones, but they only do so much when the school's HVAC system is practically ancient. The maintenance guy, Frank, told me the ducts haven't been properly cleaned since the late eighties. Can you imagine what we're breathing in every day? It's a wonder half the staff doesn't have chronic bronchitis. Frank says he keeps putting in work orders to get professionals out here to clean them, but the district keeps denying the budget request. They always say there's no money, but then they just bought brand new uniforms for the football team... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? No, my mood has been fine. Just the normal amount of stress, really.\n\nDoctor: When you do sleep at night, how many hours would you say you're typically sleeping? And do you find that you feel rested after that amount of sleep, or do you wake up feeling like you need more?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... I suppose I get about six or seven hours a night, usually. I'm not really sure if I feel rested, to be honest. It's hard to tell when my alarm goes off at 5:30 every morning. \n\nWhich is just an ungodly hour to be awake, really. But I have to get up that early because the school decided to change the bell schedule this year. We start first period at 7:15 now. 7:15! Teenagers shouldn't even be conscious at that hour, let alone trying to learn algebra. I read an article in the Times last week—or maybe it was the Post, I can't remember—anyway, it was about circadian rhythms in adolescents. It said their brains are literally wired to stay up late and sleep in. So we're fighting biology every single morning. And you can see it in their faces. Half of them are practically comatose until third period. \n\nI try to keep the lights a bit dim in my homeroom just to ease them into the day, but then the principal, Mr. Henderson, did a walkthrough last week and told me it was \"too gloomy.\" He's always on about creating a \"vibrant learning environment.\" Easy for him to say from his office with the big windows and the fancy ergonomic chair. He just ordered himself one of those standing desks, too. The ones that go up and down with a little motor. Meanwhile, my desk chair has a wobbly wheel that squeaks every time I lean to the left. I've put in three maintenance requests for it since September, but Frank—the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—he says they're backordered. Everything is backordered these days, isn't it? I tried to order some new garden shears from that catalog that comes in the mail, the one with the nice glossy pages, and they told me it would be six to eight weeks...\n\nBut sleep, yes. I get about six or seven hours. I don't know if I feel rested, I just get up and get going.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking with colleagues or family members, do people ever comment that you seem withdrawn, or that you're not expressing emotions as much as you normally would? Or have you noticed that you're having trouble organizing your thoughts or finding the right words to say?\n\nPatient: Well, nobody has really said I'm withdrawn, exactly, but... I don't know, it's like I still care about my students and my friends, obviously. But for months now, it just feels like the warmth and the drive have completely drained out of me. I just let things slide unless someone really pushes me. I haven't been going to book club, and I stepped back from the theater planning committee. Even making a simple phone call feels like climbing a mountain. \n\nSpeaking of mountains, my sister went to Colorado last month. She sent me all these pictures of the Rockies. They looked beautiful, all covered in snow. She stayed in this little cabin that had a wood-burning stove, which sounds so cozy, doesn't it? I haven't had a real fire since I lived in that apartment on 4th Street back in the nineties. It had this lovely exposed brick wall and a real fireplace, but the draft in the winter was just terrible. You had to put plastic wrap over the windows just to keep the heat in. My landlord back then, Mr. Peterson, he was entirely useless. He'd just tell you to put on a sweater. I remember I bought this thick, ugly wool sweater from a thrift shop just to survive February... \n\nBut yes, I suppose I haven't been doing as much lately. The energy just isn't there for it.\n\nDoctor: During these four months, or even before that, have you ever had a period—maybe lasting a week or two or longer—where you needed significantly less sleep than usual but still felt energetic and rested, and where you were accomplishing a lot of things or felt like you could do much more than you normally can?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. I don't think I've ever felt like I didn't need sleep, or had some sudden burst of energy to do a million things. \n\nActually, energy is something I've been thinking about a lot lately, especially with the electric bill going up again. They sent a notice last week saying the rates are increasing by eight percent starting in January. Eight percent! And they blame it on infrastructure upgrades, but you look around and the power lines in my neighborhood are still sagging into the oak trees. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's been calling the city for three years to come trim the branches away from the wires near her driveway. She's terrified that in the next big storm, a branch is going to come down and start a fire. She even bought one of those heavy-duty fire extinguishers for her garage, the kind they use in commercial kitchens. It's bright yellow, not red, which I thought was interesting. I always thought they had to be red by law, but apparently not. I saw one at the hardware store when I went to buy some weather stripping for my back door. The draft in my kitchen is just awful this time of year. Just the opposite lately, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I'm still teaching. I haven't missed any days, but... it's definitely been harder. I've had to ask some of my coworkers to cover parts of my project lessons, and I need them to help me just to get through the planning meetings. I just can't seem to hold all the pieces together the way I used to. 

And outside of work, I've completely stopped being reliable for things. The book club, the theater committee, even the neighborhood events... I just don't go anymore. Speaking of the neighborhood, they're planning the annual block party for next month, and the woman organizing it—Sarah, from three houses down—she wants everyone to chip in for a bouncy castle. A bouncy castle! I mean, most of the kids on our street are in high school now. Who is going to bounce in it? The toddlers from the next street over, I suppose. 

Sarah is always coming up with these grand ideas that don't make any sense. Last year she wanted to hire a petting zoo, but Mrs. Gable threatened to call the health department because of the llamas. Apparently, llamas spit when they get agitated, and Mrs. Gable read somewhere that their saliva has all sorts of bacteria in it. I don't know if that's true, but I certainly wouldn't want to be spat on by a llama. I saw a documentary about them once on PBS. They live high up in the Andes mountains, where the air is incredibly thin. It's amazing how animals adapt to things like that, isn't it? The narrator had this very soothing British accent. I could have listened to him read the phone book...

But yes, my work has definitely slipped. I'm just leaning on other people a lot more these days to get by.

I keep telling the principal we need a new intercom, but the school budget is always so tight. Sometimes when it's really quiet, like after the classroom finally empties out for the day... which, honestly, takes forever because the kids just linger in the hallways now. I usually bring my own French press, but I had forgotten it that morning because I was rushing to get out the door. The traffic on I-95 has been an absolute nightmare lately with all the construction... \n\nBut yes, about four months. It just sort of slowly crept up on me.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you noticed any periods where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than normal, or found yourself taking on many new projects or activities with a lot of enthusiasm?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've felt unusually energetic or anything like that. I mean, who has the energy for new projects these days anyway? I'm exhausted by the time I get home. Between the grading and the lesson planning, there's just no time. \n\nSpeaking of projects, though, the school decided to completely overhaul the science fair this year. They want us to integrate it with the arts department—some sort of \"STEAM\" initiative instead of just STEM. It's a nice idea in theory, I suppose, but the execution has been a complete disaster. The art teacher, Brenda, she's lovely, really, but she is so disorganized. We were supposed to have a joint planning meeting on Tuesday, and she completely forgot and went to get her car's oil changed instead. Which I understand, car maintenance is important. My old Subaru has been making this rattling noise every time I go over 40 miles an hour. I took it to the mechanic on Elm Street—the one with the big neon wrench sign—and he said it was the catalytic converter. Do you have any idea how much it costs to replace one of those? It's astronomical. And with the price of groceries right now, I just don't know how they expect people to manage. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's like they're made of gold. I used to bake all the time, but now I have to think twice before making a simple pound cake...\n\nBut no, I haven't been feeling energetic or taking on new things. Just trying to keep up with the usual workload.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months while you've been hearing the voice and feeling like the messages are about you, have you noticed a significant change in your mood—such as feeling persistently sad, empty, or hopeless? Or have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not crying all the time or anything like that. And I still enjoy my usual things. I still go to my book club on Thursday evenings. We're reading this historical fiction novel set in 1920s London right now. Though, honestly, Mary—she's the one who usually hosts—she always chooses these incredibly long, dense books. Last month it was an 800-page biography of some obscure British politician. Who has the time to read 800 pages? \n\nEspecially with the state of the school library right now. They decided to pull up all the old carpeting, and the dust is just everywhere in the building. My allergies have been acting up terribly because of it. I've been buying those expensive air filters for my classroom, the HEPA ones, but they only do so much when the school's HVAC system is practically ancient. The maintenance guy, Frank, told me the ducts haven't been properly cleaned since the late eighties. Can you imagine what we're breathing in every day? It's a wonder half the staff doesn't have chronic bronchitis. Frank says he keeps putting in work orders to get professionals out here to clean them, but the district keeps denying the budget request. They always say there's no money, but then they just bought brand new uniforms for the football team... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? No, my mood has been fine. Just the normal amount of stress, really.\n\nDoctor: When you do sleep at night, how many hours would you say you're typically sleeping? And do you find that you feel rested after that amount of sleep, or do you wake up feeling like you need more?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... I suppose I get about six or seven hours a night, usually. I'm not really sure if I feel rested, to be honest. It's hard to tell when my alarm goes off at 5:30 every morning. \n\nWhich is just an ungodly hour to be awake, really. But I have to get up that early because the school decided to change the bell schedule this year. We start first period at 7:15 now. 7:15! Teenagers shouldn't even be conscious at that hour, let alone trying to learn algebra. I read an article in the Times last week—or maybe it was the Post, I can't remember—anyway, it was about circadian rhythms in adolescents. It said their brains are literally wired to stay up late and sleep in. So we're fighting biology every single morning. And you can see it in their faces. Half of them are practically comatose until third period. \n\nI try to keep the lights a bit dim in my homeroom just to ease them into the day, but then the principal, Mr. Henderson, did a walkthrough last week and told me it was \"too gloomy.\" He's always on about creating a \"vibrant learning environment.\" Easy for him to say from his office with the big windows and the fancy ergonomic chair. He just ordered himself one of those standing desks, too. The ones that go up and down with a little motor. Meanwhile, my desk chair has a wobbly wheel that squeaks every time I lean to the left. I've put in three maintenance requests for it since September, but Frank—the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—he says they're backordered. Everything is backordered these days, isn't it? I tried to order some new garden shears from that catalog that comes in the mail, the one with the nice glossy pages, and they told me it would be six to eight weeks...\n\nBut sleep, yes. I get about six or seven hours. I don't know if I feel rested, I just get up and get going.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking with colleagues or family members, do people ever comment that you seem withdrawn, or that you're not expressing emotions as much as you normally would? Or have you noticed that you're having trouble organizing your thoughts or finding the right words to say?\n\nPatient: Well, nobody has really said I'm withdrawn, exactly, but... I don't know, it's like I still care about my students and my friends, obviously. But for months now, it just feels like the warmth and the drive have completely drained out of me. I just let things slide unless someone really pushes me. I haven't been going to book club, and I stepped back from the theater planning committee. Even making a simple phone call feels like climbing a mountain. \n\nSpeaking of mountains, my sister went to Colorado last month. She sent me all these pictures of the Rockies. They looked beautiful, all covered in snow. She stayed in this little cabin that had a wood-burning stove, which sounds so cozy, doesn't it? I haven't had a real fire since I lived in that apartment on 4th Street back in the nineties. It had this lovely exposed brick wall and a real fireplace, but the draft in the winter was just terrible. You had to put plastic wrap over the windows just to keep the heat in. My landlord back then, Mr. Peterson, he was entirely useless. He'd just tell you to put on a sweater. And they blame it on infrastructure upgrades, but you look around and the power lines in my neighborhood are still sagging into the oak trees. She's terrified that in the next big storm, a branch is going to come down and start a fire. She even bought one of those heavy-duty fire extinguishers for her garage, the kind they use in commercial kitchens. It's bright yellow, not red, which I thought was interesting. I always thought they had to be red by law, but apparently not. I saw one at the hardware store when I went to buy some weather stripping for my back door. The draft in my kitchen is just awful this time of year. You stand by the sink to wash dishes and your ankles absolutely freeze...\n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need to sleep. Just the opposite lately, really.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months since the messages and voices started, would you say you've been able to keep doing your job as a teacher at the same level as before, or has your work performance noticeably declined?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still teaching. I've had to ask some of my coworkers to cover parts of my project lessons, and I need them to help me just to get through the planning meetings. No, I can't say I've had anything like that. I've never been the type to get overly excited or unusually optimistic, really. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. 

Speaking of being even-keeled, my uncle Arthur was like that. Nothing ever ruffled him. He used to work at this old shoe factory down by the river. They made these really sturdy leather boots, the kind with the steel toes that construction workers wear. I remember going to visit him there once when I was a little girl, and the smell of the leather and the oil was just overwhelming. It's funny how smells stay with you, isn't it? Like the smell of chalk dust. I used to love clapping the erasers at the end of the day when I first started teaching. Now it's all whiteboards and dry-erase markers. The markers smell terrible, like chemicals, and they dry out so fast if you forget to put the cap back on tightly. I must throw away three or four markers a week because the students leave the caps off. It's so wasteful. I brought it up at the last faculty meeting, about how we need a better system for recycling the plastic casings, but the principal just looked at me like I was speaking Greek...

But anyway, no, I haven't had any days where I felt unusually good or confident like that.

I think the worries about the coded messages and hearing that woman's voice... it's been going on for about four months now. It definitely didn't happen all at once. Though, honestly, Mary—she's the one who usually hosts—she always chooses these incredibly long, dense books. Last month it was an 800-page biography of some obscure British politician. Who has the time to read 800 pages? \n\nEspecially with the state of the school library right now. They decided to pull up all the old carpeting, and the dust is just everywhere in the building. My allergies have been acting up terribly because of it. The maintenance guy, Frank, told me the ducts haven't been properly cleaned since the late eighties. Can you imagine what we're breathing in every day? It's a wonder half the staff doesn't have chronic bronchitis. Frank says he keeps putting in work orders to get professionals out here to clean them, but the district keeps denying the budget request. They always say there's no money, but then they just bought brand new uniforms for the football team... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? No, my mood has been fine. I'm not really sure if I feel rested, to be honest. It's hard to tell when my alarm goes off at 5:30 every morning. \n\nWhich is just an ungodly hour to be awake, really. But I have to get up that early because the school decided to change the bell schedule this year. We start first period at 7:15 now. 7:15! Teenagers shouldn't even be conscious at that hour, let alone trying to learn algebra. I read an article in the Times last week—or maybe it was the Post, I can't remember—anyway, it was about circadian rhythms in adolescents. It said their brains are literally wired to stay up late and sleep in. So we're fighting biology every single morning. And you can see it in their faces. Half of them are practically comatose until third period. \n\nI try to keep the lights a bit dim in my homeroom just to ease them into the day, but then the principal, Mr. Henderson, did a walkthrough last week and told me it was \"too gloomy.\" He's always on about creating a \"vibrant learning environment.\" Easy for him to say from his office with the big windows and the fancy ergonomic chair. He just ordered himself one of those standing desks, too. The ones that go up and down with a little motor. Meanwhile, my desk chair has a wobbly wheel that squeaks every time I lean to the left. I've put in three maintenance requests for it since September, but Frank—the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—he says they're backordered. Everything is backordered these days, isn't it? I tried to order some new garden shears from that catalog that comes in the mail, the one with the nice glossy pages, and they told me it would be six to eight weeks...\n\nBut sleep, yes. The energy just isn't there for it.\n\nDoctor: During these four months, or even before that, have you ever had a period—maybe lasting a week or two or longer—where you needed significantly less sleep than usual but still felt energetic and rested, and where you were accomplishing a lot of things or felt like you could do much more than you normally can?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. I don't think I've ever felt like I didn't need sleep, or had some sudden burst of energy to do a million things. \n\nActually, energy is something I've been thinking about a lot lately, especially with the electric bill going up again. They sent a notice last week saying the rates are increasing by eight percent starting in January. Eight percent! And they blame it on infrastructure upgrades, but you look around and the power lines in my neighborhood are still sagging into the oak trees. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's been calling the city for three years to come trim the branches away from the wires near her driveway. She's terrified that in the next big storm, a branch is going to come down and start a fire. She even bought one of those heavy-duty fire extinguishers for her garage, the kind they use in commercial kitchens. It's bright yellow, not red, which I thought was interesting. I always thought they had to be red by law, but apparently not. I saw one at the hardware store when I went to buy some weather stripping for my back door. The draft in my kitchen is just awful this time of year. You stand by the sink to wash dishes and your ankles absolutely freeze...\n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need to sleep. I've had to ask some of my coworkers to cover parts of my project lessons, and I need them to help me just to get through the planning meetings. I just can't seem to hold all the pieces together the way I used to. \n\nAnd outside of work, I've completely stopped being reliable for things. The book club, the theater committee, even the neighborhood events... I just don't go anymore. Speaking of the neighborhood, they're planning the annual block party for next month, and the woman organizing it—Sarah, from three houses down—she wants everyone to chip in for a bouncy castle. A bouncy castle! I mean, most of the kids on our street are in high school now. Who is going to bounce in it? The toddlers from the next street over, I suppose. \n\nSarah is always coming up with these grand ideas that don't make any sense. Last year she wanted to hire a petting zoo, but Mrs. Gable threatened to call the health department because of the llamas. Apparently, llamas spit when they get agitated, and Mrs. Gable read somewhere that their saliva has all sorts of bacteria in it. I don't know if that's true, but I certainly wouldn't want to be spat on by a llama. I saw a documentary about them once on PBS. They live high up in the Andes mountains, where the air is incredibly thin. It's amazing how animals adapt to things like that, isn't it? The narrator had this very soothing British accent. I could have listened to him read the phone book...\n\nBut yes, my work has definitely slipped. I'm just leaning on other people a lot more these days to get by.\n\nDoctor: Before these four months when things started to decline, looking back over the past year or two, was there ever a time when you felt unusually optimistic, confident, or in an especially good mood for several days or longer—not just a normal good day, but something that felt different or more intense than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I've had anything like that. I've never been the type to get overly excited or unusually optimistic, really. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. \n\nSpeaking of being even-keeled, my uncle Arthur was like that. Nothing ever ruffled him. He used to work at this old shoe factory down by the river. They made these really sturdy leather boots, the kind with the steel toes that construction workers wear. I remember going to visit him there once when I was a little girl, and the smell of the leather and the oil was just overwhelming. It's funny how smells stay with you, isn't it? Like the smell of chalk dust. I used to love clapping the erasers at the end of the day when I first started teaching. Now it's all whiteboards and dry-erase markers. The markers smell terrible, like chemicals, and they dry out so fast if you forget to put the cap back on tightly. I must throw away three or four markers a week because the students leave the caps off. It's so wasteful. I brought it up at the last faculty meeting, about how we need a better system for recycling the plastic casings, but the principal just looked at me like I was speaking Greek...\n\nBut anyway, no, I haven't had any days where I felt unusually good or confident like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I wouldn't say I've been confused about what's real. I mean, the messages are real, the voice is real... I know what's happening around me. I don't get disoriented or anything like that. 

Like yesterday, I was at the grocery store—the one over on Elm Street, because the one closer to my house stopped carrying my favorite brand of decaf coffee. Even when my colleagues gently show me that the emails were just standard memos sent to everyone on the staff list, I still feel like there's a hidden meaning in them meant specifically for me.\n\nDoctor: Besides these feelings about the emails and announcements being about you, have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people around you couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Sometimes when it's really quiet, like after the classroom finally empties out for the day... which, honestly, takes forever because the kids just linger in the hallways now. They don't go straight to their lockers or the buses like they used to. They just stand around staring at their phones. I had to confiscate three phones just during my second-period class yesterday. It's school policy, but the parents get so upset with us when we take them away, as if we're the ones breaking the rules! I had a mother email me last month absolutely furious because her daughter couldn't text her during a quiz. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, hearing things. Yes, when the room is finally empty, or when I'm just in the quiet of my own house, I do hear a woman's voice. She'll just say my name, or make a short comment about what I'm doing at that moment. It sounds so completely clear that I actually answer her out loud before I realize no one is there. It happens when I'm grading, or cooking... I make a really wonderful pot roast, by the way. It's my grandmother's recipe. She used to cook it in this heavy cast-iron Dutch oven that I inherited from her. Cast iron is so heavy and hard to clean, though, you can't use regular dish soap on it or it completely ruins the seasoning...\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings that the emails and announcements were about you, and when did you first hear the woman's voice? Was this something that came on suddenly over a few days, or has it been building up gradually over weeks or months?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... I think the worries about the coded messages and hearing that woman's voice... it's been going on for about four months now. It definitely didn't happen all at once. It was more of a gradual thing, building up over the semester. \n\nWhich, honestly, has been the longest semester of my life. The administration decided to change our entire grading software right in the middle of October. Can you imagine? Just as midterms were approaching! We all had to sit through this dreadful four-hour professional development seminar on a Friday afternoon to learn how to use it. The presenter just read off his slides the entire time in this monotone voice. I had to drink three cups of that terrible teachers' lounge coffee just to stay awake. You know the kind—it tastes like it's been sitting on the burner since 1998 and it's practically sludge at the bottom of the pot. I usually bring my own French press, but I had forgotten it that morning because I was rushing to get out the door. The traffic on I-95 has been an absolute nightmare lately with all the construction... \n\nBut yes, about four months. It just sort of slowly crept up on me.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you noticed any periods where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than normal, or found yourself taking on many new projects or activities with a lot of enthusiasm?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've felt unusually energetic or anything like that. I mean, who has the energy for new projects these days anyway? I'm exhausted by the time I get home. Between the grading and the lesson planning, there's just no time. \n\nSpeaking of projects, though, the school decided to completely overhaul the science fair this year. They want us to integrate it with the arts department—some sort of \"STEAM\" initiative instead of just STEM. It's a nice idea in theory, I suppose, but the execution has been a complete disaster. The art teacher, Brenda, she's lovely, really, but she is so disorganized. We were supposed to have a joint planning meeting on Tuesday, and she completely forgot and went to get her car's oil changed instead. Which I understand, car maintenance is important. My old Subaru has been making this rattling noise every time I go over 40 miles an hour. I took it to the mechanic on Elm Street—the one with the big neon wrench sign—and he said it was the catalytic converter. Do you have any idea how much it costs to replace one of those? It's astronomical. And with the price of groceries right now, I just don't know how they expect people to manage. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's like they're made of gold. I used to bake all the time, but now I have to think twice before making a simple pound cake...\n\nBut no, I haven't been feeling energetic or taking on new things. Just trying to keep up with the usual workload.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months while you've been hearing the voice and feeling like the messages are about you, have you noticed a significant change in your mood—such as feeling persistently sad, empty, or hopeless? Or have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not crying all the time or anything like that. And I still enjoy my usual things. I still go to my book club on Thursday evenings. We're reading this historical fiction novel set in 1920s London right now. Though, honestly, Mary—she's the one who usually hosts—she always chooses these incredibly long, dense books. Last month it was an 800-page biography of some obscure British politician. Who has the time to read 800 pages? \n\nEspecially with the state of the school library right now. They decided to pull up all the old carpeting, and the dust is just everywhere in the building. My allergies have been acting up terribly because of it. I've been buying those expensive air filters for my classroom, the HEPA ones, but they only do so much when the school's HVAC system is practically ancient. The maintenance guy, Frank, told me the ducts haven't been properly cleaned since the late eighties. Can you imagine what we're breathing in every day? It's a wonder half the staff doesn't have chronic bronchitis. Frank says he keeps putting in work orders to get professionals out here to clean them, but the district keeps denying the budget request. They always say there's no money, but then they just bought brand new uniforms for the football team... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? No, my mood has been fine. Just the normal amount of stress, really.\n\nDoctor: When you do sleep at night, how many hours would you say you're typically sleeping? And do you find that you feel rested after that amount of sleep, or do you wake up feeling like you need more?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... I suppose I get about six or seven hours a night, usually. I'm not really sure if I feel rested, to be honest. It's hard to tell when my alarm goes off at 5:30 every morning. \n\nWhich is just an ungodly hour to be awake, really. But I have to get up that early because the school decided to change the bell schedule this year. We start first period at 7:15 now. 7:15! Teenagers shouldn't even be conscious at that hour, let alone trying to learn algebra. I read an article in the Times last week—or maybe it was the Post, I can't remember—anyway, it was about circadian rhythms in adolescents. It said their brains are literally wired to stay up late and sleep in. So we're fighting biology every single morning. And you can see it in their faces. Half of them are practically comatose until third period. \n\nI try to keep the lights a bit dim in my homeroom just to ease them into the day, but then the principal, Mr. Henderson, did a walkthrough last week and told me it was \"too gloomy.\" He's always on about creating a \"vibrant learning environment.\" Easy for him to say from his office with the big windows and the fancy ergonomic chair. He just ordered himself one of those standing desks, too. The ones that go up and down with a little motor. Meanwhile, my desk chair has a wobbly wheel that squeaks every time I lean to the left. I've put in three maintenance requests for it since September, but Frank—the maintenance guy I mentioned earlier—he says they're backordered. Everything is backordered these days, isn't it? I tried to order some new garden shears from that catalog that comes in the mail, the one with the nice glossy pages, and they told me it would be six to eight weeks...\n\nBut sleep, yes. I get about six or seven hours. I don't know if I feel rested, I just get up and get going.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking with colleagues or family members, do people ever comment that you seem withdrawn, or that you're not expressing emotions as much as you normally would? Or have you noticed that you're having trouble organizing your thoughts or finding the right words to say?\n\nPatient: Well, nobody has really said I'm withdrawn, exactly, but... I don't know, it's like I still care about my students and my friends, obviously. But for months now, it just feels like the warmth and the drive have completely drained out of me. I just let things slide unless someone really pushes me. I haven't been going to book club, and I stepped back from the theater planning committee. Even making a simple phone call feels like climbing a mountain. \n\nSpeaking of mountains, my sister went to Colorado last month. She sent me all these pictures of the Rockies. They looked beautiful, all covered in snow. She stayed in this little cabin that had a wood-burning stove, which sounds so cozy, doesn't it? I haven't had a real fire since I lived in that apartment on 4th Street back in the nineties. It had this lovely exposed brick wall and a real fireplace, but the draft in the winter was just terrible. You had to put plastic wrap over the windows just to keep the heat in. My landlord back then, Mr. Peterson, he was entirely useless. He'd just tell you to put on a sweater. I remember I bought this thick, ugly wool sweater from a thrift shop just to survive February... \n\nBut yes, I suppose I haven't been doing as much lately. The energy just isn't there for it.\n\nDoctor: During these four months, or even before that, have you ever had a period—maybe lasting a week or two or longer—where you needed significantly less sleep than usual but still felt energetic and rested, and where you were accomplishing a lot of things or felt like you could do much more than you normally can?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. I don't think I've ever felt like I didn't need sleep, or had some sudden burst of energy to do a million things. \n\nActually, energy is something I've been thinking about a lot lately, especially with the electric bill going up again. They sent a notice last week saying the rates are increasing by eight percent starting in January. Eight percent! And they blame it on infrastructure upgrades, but you look around and the power lines in my neighborhood are still sagging into the oak trees. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's been calling the city for three years to come trim the branches away from the wires near her driveway. She's terrified that in the next big storm, a branch is going to come down and start a fire. She even bought one of those heavy-duty fire extinguishers for her garage, the kind they use in commercial kitchens. It's bright yellow, not red, which I thought was interesting. I always thought they had to be red by law, but apparently not. I saw one at the hardware store when I went to buy some weather stripping for my back door. The draft in my kitchen is just awful this time of year. You stand by the sink to wash dishes and your ankles absolutely freeze...\n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need to sleep. Just the opposite lately, really.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months since the messages and voices started, would you say you've been able to keep doing your job as a teacher at the same level as before, or has your work performance noticeably declined?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still teaching. I haven't missed any days, but... it's definitely been harder. I've had to ask some of my coworkers to cover parts of my project lessons, and I need them to help me just to get through the planning meetings. I just can't seem to hold all the pieces together the way I used to. \n\nAnd outside of work, I've completely stopped being reliable for things. The book club, the theater committee, even the neighborhood events... I just don't go anymore. Speaking of the neighborhood, they're planning the annual block party for next month, and the woman organizing it—Sarah, from three houses down—she wants everyone to chip in for a bouncy castle. A bouncy castle! I mean, most of the kids on our street are in high school now. Who is going to bounce in it? The toddlers from the next street over, I suppose. \n\nSarah is always coming up with these grand ideas that don't make any sense. Last year she wanted to hire a petting zoo, but Mrs. Gable threatened to call the health department because of the llamas. Apparently, llamas spit when they get agitated, and Mrs. Gable read somewhere that their saliva has all sorts of bacteria in it. I don't know if that's true, but I certainly wouldn't want to be spat on by a llama. I saw a documentary about them once on PBS. They live high up in the Andes mountains, where the air is incredibly thin. It's amazing how animals adapt to things like that, isn't it? The narrator had this very soothing British accent. I could have listened to him read the phone book...\n\nBut yes, my work has definitely slipped. I'm just leaning on other people a lot more these days to get by.\n\nDoctor: Before these four months when things started to decline, looking back over the past year or two, was there ever a time when you felt unusually optimistic, confident, or in an especially good mood for several days or longer—not just a normal good day, but something that felt different or more intense than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I've had anything like that. I've never been the type to get overly excited or unusually optimistic, really. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. \n\nSpeaking of being even-keeled, my uncle Arthur was like that. Nothing ever ruffled him. He used to work at this old shoe factory down by the river. They made these really sturdy leather boots, the kind with the steel toes that construction workers wear. I remember going to visit him there once when I was a little girl, and the smell of the leather and the oil was just overwhelming. It's funny how smells stay with you, isn't it? Like the smell of chalk dust. I used to love clapping the erasers at the end of the day when I first started teaching. Now it's all whiteboards and dry-erase markers. The markers smell terrible, like chemicals, and they dry out so fast if you forget to put the cap back on tightly. I must throw away three or four markers a week because the students leave the caps off. It's so wasteful. I brought it up at the last faculty meeting, about how we need a better system for recycling the plastic casings, but the principal just looked at me like I was speaking Greek...\n\nBut anyway, no, I haven't had any days where I felt unusually good or confident like that.\n\nDoctor: During these four months when you've been hearing the voice and feeling like the messages are about you, have there been times when you felt confused about what's real, or times when you couldn't tell what was actually happening around you versus what wasn't?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've been confused about what's real. I mean, the messages are real, the voice is real... I know what's happening around me. I don't get disoriented or anything like that. \n\nLike yesterday, I was at the grocery store—the one over on Elm Street, because the one closer to my house stopped carrying my favorite brand of decaf coffee. It's the one in the green foil bag, you know? Anyway, I was in the produce section, and I saw they had these enormous grapefruits. Just massive. I haven't seen grapefruits that big since I went to Florida with my sister, oh, it must have been ten years ago. We stayed at this little motel near the beach, and the owner had a grapefruit tree right by the pool. Every morning he'd pick a fresh one for us. It was so sweet you didn't even need sugar. My sister always puts sugar on her grapefruit, which I think ruins the whole point of eating citrus. She has a terrible sweet tooth, always has. When we were kids, she used to sneak down to the kitchen and eat brown sugar straight out of the box with a spoon. My mother caught her once and grounded her for a week...\n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any trouble telling what's real. I know exactly what's going on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. When we were kids, she used to sneak down to the kitchen and eat brown sugar straight out of the box with a spoon. My mother caught her once and grounded her for a week...\n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't had any trouble telling what's real. I know exactly what's going on.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen your colleagues show you that the emails were sent to everyone on the staff list, and you still feel like they're about you—do you recognize that your colleagues might be right, or do you remain convinced that there's a hidden meaning meant just for you despite what they're showing you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can’t shake the feeling that certain staff emails and morning announcements at school are really coded messages about me, even when my colleagues gently show me they were sent to everyone.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, they show me the "To:" line, and I see that it says "All Staff," but... it doesn't change how it feels. I came in because I just can't shake the feeling that those emails, and even the morning announcements over the loudspeaker, are really coded messages about me. Even when the other teachers are being very gentle and pointing out that everyone got the same email, I just know there's something hidden in there meant for me. 

Speaking of the morning announcements, the speaker in my classroom has this awful static sound right before the principal starts talking. It’s like a pop and then a hiss. It reminds me of my father’s old ham radio setup he had in the basement when I was growing up. He used to spend hours down there, twisting the dials, trying to talk to people all over the world. He had this giant map on the wall with little red pins for every country he’d made contact with. He even talked to someone in Antarctica once! Can you imagine? Being stuck in the freezing cold at a research station at the bottom of the world. I don't think I could handle the cold. I get chilly just walking from my car to the school building in November. I had to buy this heavy wool coat last year, but it's so bulky I can barely bend my arms to carry my grading bag... 

But yes, even when they show me the email list, I'm still convinced the messages are about me. I just can't shake it.

Or have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not crying all the time or anything like that. And I still enjoy my usual things. I still go to my book club on Thursday evenings. We're reading this historical fiction novel set in 1920s London right now. Though, honestly, Mary—she's the one who usually hosts—she always chooses these incredibly long, dense books. Last month it was an 800-page biography of some obscure British politician. Who has the time to read 800 pages? \n\nEspecially with the state of the school library right now. They decided to pull up all the old carpeting, and the dust is just everywhere in the building. It's a wonder half the staff doesn't have chronic bronchitis. They looked beautiful, all covered in snow. She stayed in this little cabin that had a wood-burning stove, which sounds so cozy, doesn't it? The book club, the theater committee, even the neighborhood events... I just don't go anymore. Speaking of the neighborhood, they're planning the annual block party for next month, and the woman organizing it—Sarah, from three houses down—she wants everyone to chip in for a bouncy castle. A bouncy castle! I mean, most of the kids on our street are in high school now. Who is going to bounce in it? The toddlers from the next street over, I suppose. \n\nSarah is always coming up with these grand ideas that don't make any sense. Last year she wanted to hire a petting zoo, but Mrs. Gable threatened to call the health department because of the llamas. Apparently, llamas spit when they get agitated, and Mrs. Gable read somewhere that their saliva has all sorts of bacteria in it. I don't know if that's true, but I certainly wouldn't want to be spat on by a llama. I saw a documentary about them once on PBS. They live high up in the Andes mountains, where the air is incredibly thin. It's amazing how animals adapt to things like that, isn't it? The narrator had this very soothing British accent. I could have listened to him read the phone book...\n\nBut yes, my work has definitely slipped. I'm just leaning on other people a lot more these days to get by.\n\nDoctor: Before these four months when things started to decline, looking back over the past year or two, was there ever a time when you felt unusually optimistic, confident, or in an especially good mood for several days or longer—not just a normal good day, but something that felt different or more intense than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I've had anything like that. I've never been the type to get overly excited or unusually optimistic, really. I'm usually pretty even-keeled. \n\nSpeaking of being even-keeled, my uncle Arthur was like that. Nothing ever ruffled him. He used to work at this old shoe factory down by the river. They made these really sturdy leather boots, the kind with the steel toes that construction workers wear. The markers smell terrible, like chemicals, and they dry out so fast if you forget to put the cap back on tightly. I must throw away three or four markers a week because the students leave the caps off. It's so wasteful. I mean, the messages are real, the voice is real... I know what's happening around me. I don't get disoriented or anything like that. \n\nLike yesterday, I was at the grocery store—the one over on Elm Street, because the one closer to my house stopped carrying my favorite brand of decaf coffee. I haven't seen grapefruits that big since I went to Florida with my sister, oh, it must have been ten years ago. We stayed at this little motel near the beach, and the owner had a grapefruit tree right by the pool. Every morning he'd pick a fresh one for us. It was so sweet you didn't even need sugar. My sister always puts sugar on her grapefruit, which I think ruins the whole point of eating citrus. She has a terrible sweet tooth, always has.
